<details id="proof-stmt-lem-return-expansion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-return-expansion</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#lem:hitting-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hitting-energy">22</a> gives $$q_i\le Q_{ii}+2\langle Q\eta,g\rangle
                  -\langle g,A_Qg\rangle.$$ Every $Q^n\eta$ is a nonnegative mass of total at most one. Consequently $\|v\|_\infty\le K-1$ and $\|y\|_\infty\le K$; the slightly weaker displayed bound also covers all later uses. The contraction and symmetry of $Q$ give $E_y\ge0$. The finite telescoping identity $A_Qv=Q\eta-Q^K\eta$ shows that substitution of $g=v+y$ leaves the linear term $2\langle Q^K\eta,y\rangle$. To compute the constant term, put $a_n=\langle\eta,Q^n\eta\rangle$. By symmetry, $$\begin{aligned}
 Q_{ii}+2\langle Q\eta,v\rangle-\langle v,A_Qv\rangle
 &=a_1+\langle v,Q\eta\rangle+\langle v,Q^K\eta\rangle\\
 &=a_1+\sum_{n=1}^{K-1}a_{n+1}+\sum_{n=1}^{K-1}a_{n+K}
   =\sum_{n=1}^{2K-1}a_n.
\end{aligned}$$ This proves <a href="#eq:return-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:return-expansion">[eq:return-expansion]</a>. No infinite Green series or assumption of eventual boundary absorption is used. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-long-tail"></a>
<div id="prop:long-tail" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:long-tail"></a>
**Proposition 26** (Long-return bound). *For every fixed integer $K\ge1$, as $\beta\to\infty$, $$\label{eq:long-tail}
 \mathbb E_P\bigl[2\langle Q^K\eta,y\rangle-E_y\bigr]
 \le C K^{-1/2}\beta^{-3/2}+o_K(\beta^{-3/2}).$$ Here $C$ depends only on the fixed interaction, spin, and geometric choices, and is independent of $K$. The error has the uniformity specified above.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-long-tail"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-long-tail</summary>

<a id="eq:tail-suffix"></a>
*Proof.* Set $w=Q^{K-1}\eta$ and $I=[\beta/3,2\beta/3]$. Then $$2\langle Q^K\eta,y\rangle
 =\langle U_+w,y\rangle+\langle U_-w,y\rangle.$$ For $d\in\{+,-\}$ and almost every $u\in I$, split the last traversal as in Lemma <a href="#lem:suffix-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:suffix-energy">23</a>: $$U_d=B_d(u)C_d(u),\qquad Y_d(u)=B_d(u)^*y.$$ Both vectors in the resulting pairing $$\langle U_dw,y\rangle=\langle C_d(u)w,Y_d(u)\rangle$$ are on the same hole layer at physical time $t_d(u)=\theta+u$ or $\theta-u\pmod\beta$. The suffix remains killed only at the boundary. With zero extension to the slice graph, Lemma <a href="#lem:suffix-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:suffix-energy">23</a> gives $$\label{eq:tail-suffix}
 \|Y_d(u)\|_\infty\le K+1,\qquad
 \int_I\mathcal D_{t_d(u)}(Y_d(u))\,du\le E_y.$$ In particular the energy on the right is the original one-period quadratic form, with no localization error.

<a id="eq:tail-localization"></a>
Localize the mass in the first argument throughout its propagation from $i$. Namely, put $$Q_R=\tfrac12(U_{+,R}+U_{-,R}),\qquad
 \mu_d(u)=C_{d,R}(u)Q_R^{K-1}\eta,$$ where the subscript $R$ means killing on leaving $\mathfrak B_R(z)$, in addition to boundary killing. Expanding the powers of $Q$ and $Q_R$ gives convex combinations of matching direction words. Lemma <a href="#lem:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:localization">20</a>, applied to the whole history of each word, therefore yields $$\label{eq:tail-localization}
 \mathbb E_P\|C_d(u)w-\mu_d(u)\|_1\le C_K e^{-c\beta},
 \qquad u\in I.$$

<a id="eq:tail-discarded"></a>
Let $G$ denote the event that the exposure is good for $z,R$, and retain only regular split slices in the time average $$F_d=\frac1{|I|}\int_I
  \mathbf 1_{G}\mathbf 1_{\{t_d(u)\text{ is regular for }z,R\}}
       \langle\mu_d(u),Y_d(u)\rangle\,du.$$ The original pairing is independent of $u$. Since $\mu_d(u)$ has mass at most one, <a href="#eq:tail-suffix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-suffix">[eq:tail-suffix]</a>, <a href="#eq:tail-localization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-localization">[eq:tail-localization]</a>, <a href="#eq:irregularity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:irregularity">[eq:irregularity]</a>, and <a href="#eq:good-exposure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:good-exposure">[eq:good-exposure]</a> imply $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_P\bigl|\langle U_dw,y\rangle-F_d\bigr|
 &\le(K+1)\left(C_K e^{-c\beta}+\mathbb P_P(G^c)
    +\frac1{|I|}\int_I
       \mathbb P_P(t_d(u)\text{ is not regular for }z,R)\,du\right)
       \notag\\
 &=o_K(\beta^{-3/2}).\label{eq:tail-discarded}
\end{aligned}$$ These are unconditional expectations under the exposure law. In particular no independence between the propagated mass, the suffix, and the regularity events is asserted or needed.

On a retained slice, every component of $\mu_d(u)$ has elapsed propagation length $$b=(K-1)\beta+u\ge(K-\tfrac23)\beta\ge\tfrac13K\beta.$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:diffusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:diffusion">21</a> and convexity of the norm give $\|\mu_d(u)\|_2\le C\tau^{-3/4}$, with a constant independent of $K$. The mass is at most one and is supported over $\mathfrak B_R(z)$. Since $1\le\tau\le R$, all hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:spread-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:spread-energy">16</a> hold. Denote its constant for this fixed norm bound by $C_{\rm sp}$; it too is independent of $K$. Hence $$|\langle\mu_d(u),Y_d(u)\rangle|
 \le\bigl(C_{\rm sp}\tau^{-1/2}
             \mathcal D_{t_d(u)}(Y_d(u))\bigr)^{1/2}.$$ Cauchy–Schwarz in $u$, followed by <a href="#eq:tail-suffix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-suffix">[eq:tail-suffix]</a>, gives $$|F_d|\le
 \left(\frac{3C_{\rm sp}\tau^{-1/2}}{\beta}\right)^{1/2}E_y^{1/2}.$$ This also holds on $G^c$, where $F_d=0$. Completing the square therefore gives, pointwise in the exposure, $$F_++F_--E_y
 \le\frac{3C_{\rm sp}\tau^{-1/2}}{\beta}
 =3C_{\rm sp}K^{-1/2}\beta^{-3/2}.$$ Take expectations and add the two errors in <a href="#eq:tail-discarded" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-discarded">[eq:tail-discarded]</a>. This proves <a href="#eq:long-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:long-tail">[eq:long-tail]</a> and identifies a leading constant that is independent of $K$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-singleton-bound"></a>
<div id="cor:singleton-bound" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:singleton-return"></a>
<a id="eq:free-slot"></a>
**Corollary 27** (Uniform singleton sparsity). *For every sufficiently large $\beta$, uniformly in all allowed pin sets $E$, cubes, cuts, and slot-time points $i\notin E$, $$\label{eq:singleton-return}
 r(E,\{i\})\le C\beta^{-3/2}.$$ Consequently, for every allowed pin set $D$ and every slot-time point $j$ at a deterministic time, $$\label{eq:free-slot}
 \mathbb P_D(j\in\mathcal B_D)\le C\beta^{-3/2}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-singleton-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-singleton-bound</summary>

*Proof.* Take $K=1$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:return-expansion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:return-expansion">25</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:long-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:long-tail">26</a>. By the fresh-walk identity, $$r(E,\{i\})=\mathbb E_Pq_i
 \le\mathbb E_P Q_{ii}+C\beta^{-3/2}+o(\beta^{-3/2}).$$ The two one-turn localization losses are at most $Ce^{-c\beta}$ in expectation. On a good exposure, the localized diagonal is at most $C\beta^{-3/2}$ by Proposition <a href="#prop:diffusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:diffusion">21</a>; on its complement it is at most one. The complement has probability $o(\beta^{-3/2})$ by <a href="#eq:good-exposure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:good-exposure">[eq:good-exposure]</a>. This bounds $\mathbb E_PQ_{ii}$ and proves <a href="#eq:singleton-return" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:singleton-return">[eq:singleton-return]</a>. For $j\notin D$, apply this estimate with $E=D$, using the time of $j$ as the cut, and use Proposition <a href="#prop:pin-identities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pin-identities">5</a>: $$\mathbb P_D(j\in\mathcal B_D)=2d_D(j)
 =\frac{2r(D,\{j\})}{1+r(D,\{j\})}
 \le2r(D,\{j\}).$$ For $j\in D$, the left side is zero. ◻

</details>

The estimate <a href="#eq:free-slot" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-slot">[eq:free-slot]</a> is obtained only after closing the sparse-pin induction. It will now control encounters between exposed strands and an independent ideal path, allowing the explicit finite sum in <a href="#eq:return-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:return-expansion">[eq:return-expansion]</a> to be evaluated sharply.

<a id="sec:ideal"></a>
### Finite returns and the Bloch coefficient

We identify the finite return terms using the walk with every slot available. Its site motion uses all interaction bonds, so its diffusion matrix is $D_{S,J}$, rather than a matrix determined by the three bonds chosen for the geometric estimates. The comparison has two steps: a coupling makes the expected mass error $o_K(1)$, and splitting a return at its midpoint multiplies this error by the diffusion bound $O(\beta^{-3/2})$.

Fix $K$, $E$, $i\notin E$, $P=E\cup\{i\}$, and $R=R_K$ as in Section <a href="#sec:returns" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:returns">5</a>. Write $x$ for the site of $i$ and $z=\bar x$. For a direction word $\mathbf d=(d_1,\ldots,d_n)\in\{+,-\}^n$, traversed with $d_1$ first, define $$W(\mathbf d)=U_{d_n}\cdots U_{d_1},\qquad
 W_R(\mathbf d)=U_{d_n,R}\cdots U_{d_1,R}.$$ The factors are formed in one fixed exposure; each traversal then uses fresh transition randomness. The ideal kernels have all slots available, exchange rate $J(y-x)/2$ on each slot edge, and fresh uniform site permutations at every seam. The reverse factors use the inverse permutations and the reversed order of operations. Denote the full ideal word over $\mathbb Z^3$, with no killing, by $\widehat W_\infty(\mathbf d)$. Denote its version killed outside $\Lambda^\circ$ and outside $\mathfrak B_R(z)$ by $\widehat W_R(\mathbf d)$. Ideal and exposed kernels assign each instantaneous operation to the same endpoint and use the same matching layers. The ideal kernels are deterministic transition matrices, with their fresh randomness already averaged.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### The full interaction kernel

<a id="eq:ideal-fourier"></a>
The site of a full ideal path jumps by $v$ at rate $\ell J(v)/2=SJ(v)$. Its total jump rate is the finite constant $\lambda_J=S\sum_vJ(v)$. Neither a seam nor a reversal changes this site generator. With $$\phi(k)=S\sum_vJ(v)(1-\cos(k\cdot v)),\qquad
 \kappa=\frac1{(4\pi)^{3/2}\sqrt{\det D_{S,J}}},$$ the site transition kernel is $$\label{eq:ideal-fourier}
 p_t(y,w)=\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^3}
     e^{\mathrm i k\cdot(w-y)}e^{-t\phi(k)}\,
                         \frac{dk}{(2\pi)^3}.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-ideal-kernel"></a>
<div id="lem:ideal-kernel" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:ideal-site-bounds"></a>
<a id="eq:ideal-diagonal"></a>
<a id="eq:ideal-killed-upper"></a>
<a id="eq:ideal-killed-interior"></a>
**Lemma 28** (Ideal return coefficient). *The full site kernel satisfies $$\label{eq:ideal-site-bounds}
 \sup_{y,w}p_t(y,w)\le C(1+t)^{-3/2},\qquad
 p_t(x,x)=t^{-3/2}(\kappa+o(1)).$$ The first estimate holds for all $t\ge0$; the second is as $t\to\infty$. For every fixed $K$, every $1\le n\le2K$, and every direction word of length $n$, $$\label{eq:ideal-diagonal}
 (\widehat W_\infty(\mathbf d))_{ii}
 =\ell^{-1}p_{n\beta}(x,x)
 =\beta^{-3/2}\bigl((\kappa/\ell)n^{-3/2}+o_K(1)\bigr).$$ The error is uniform in the word, cut, and initial slot. Always, $$\label{eq:ideal-killed-upper}
 0\le(\widehat W_R(\mathbf d))_{ii}
 \le(\widehat W_\infty(\mathbf d))_{ii}.$$ If $\mathfrak B_R(z)\subseteq\Lambda^\circ$, then $$\label{eq:ideal-killed-interior}
 0\le(\widehat W_\infty(\mathbf d))_{ii}
          -(\widehat W_R(\mathbf d))_{ii}
 \le C_K e^{-c\beta}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-ideal-kernel"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-ideal-kernel</summary>

*Proof.* At a zero of $\phi$, each $k\cdot v$ with $J(v)>0$ belongs to $2\pi\mathbb Z$. Since these $v$ generate $\mathbb Z^3$, this forces $k\in2\pi\mathbb Z^3$. Thus zero is the only zero in the integration cube. Moreover, $$\phi(k)=k^{\mathsf T}D_{S,J}k+O(|k|^4).$$ The matrix is positive definite because the support spans $\mathbb R^3$. Compactness away from zero now gives $\phi(k)\ge c|k|^2$ throughout $[-\pi,\pi]^3$. Taking absolute values in <a href="#eq:ideal-fourier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ideal-fourier">[eq:ideal-fourier]</a> proves the heat kernel upper bound, including $t\le1$ by enlarging the constant. For the diagonal, substitute $k=u/\sqrt t$ and use dominated convergence with the bound $e^{-c|u|^2}$. The limiting integral is $$\int_{\mathbb R^3}e^{-u^{\mathsf T}D_{S,J}u}
                     \frac{du}{(2\pi)^3}
 =\frac1{(4\pi)^{3/2}\sqrt{\det D_{S,J}}}.$$ This proves <a href="#eq:ideal-site-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ideal-site-bounds">[eq:ideal-site-bounds]</a>.

Averaging a uniform site permutation makes the slot index uniform conditional on the site. The inverse of a uniform permutation has the same property. Every complete traversal contains one seam: if the cut is zero, it is at the end of a forward traversal and at the start of a reverse traversal. Free exchange transitions preserve this conditional uniformity, since the rate to each target slot is the same and the exit rate does not depend on the starting slot. Thus after the first seam, the slot index is uniform conditional on the site. The site process runs for total elapsed time $n\beta$, proving the exact equality in <a href="#eq:ideal-diagonal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ideal-diagonal">[eq:ideal-diagonal]</a>. In particular, seam factors at a forward and reverse junction are two fresh averages; they do not cancel as fixed permutations would. The local limit is uniform over the finite set $1\le n\le2K$.

Killing restricts a path event, proving <a href="#eq:ideal-killed-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ideal-killed-upper">[eq:ideal-killed-upper]</a>. Under the interior hypothesis it cannot act before the full ideal path leaves $\mathfrak B_R(z)$. The ideal localization estimate in Lemma <a href="#lem:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:localization">20</a>, with the same radius and at most $2K$ traversals, bounds this exit probability by $C_K e^{-c\beta}$. This proves <a href="#eq:ideal-killed-interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ideal-killed-interior">[eq:ideal-killed-interior]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Comparison in one exposure

<a id="stmt-prop-finite-word"></a>
<div id="prop:finite-word" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:finite-word"></a>
**Proposition 29** (Finite direction words). *For every fixed $K$, $$\label{eq:finite-word}
 \left|\mathbb E_P[(W_R(\mathbf d))_{ii}]
                 -(\widehat W_R(\mathbf d))_{ii}\right|
       =o_K(\beta^{-3/2}).$$ The error is uniform over $1\le n\le2K$, all direction words of length $n$, all cubes, deterministic cuts and slots, and all allowed pin sets $E$ with arbitrary finitely many extra pins. The product inside the expectation is formed before averaging over the exposure.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-finite-word"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-finite-word</summary>

*Proof.* *Midpoint reduction.* Split the elapsed word at time $n\beta/2$, writing $$W_R=\mathsf B\mathsf C,\qquad
 \widehat W_R=\widehat{\mathsf B}\widehat{\mathsf C}.$$ Here $\mathsf C$ is the starting portion. If the split is at an instantaneous operation, assign that operation to one portion exactly once. At a junction retain the order of the individual seam factors. For $\eta=\mathbf 1_{\{i\}}$, put $$\mu_1=\mathsf C\eta,\quad \mu_2=\mathsf B^*\eta,\qquad
 \widehat\mu_1=\widehat{\mathsf C}\eta,\quad
 \widehat\mu_2=\widehat{\mathsf B}^*\eta.$$ After extending hole vectors by zero, all four are nonnegative masses of total at most one on the same full slot layer. Consequently <a id="eq:midpoint-pairings"></a>$$\label{eq:midpoint-pairings}
 (W_R)_{ii}=\langle\mu_1,\mu_2\rangle,\qquad
 (\widehat W_R)_{ii}=\langle\widehat\mu_1,\widehat\mu_2\rangle.$$ The second mass is propagated backward from the terminal copy of $i$; it is not a separately sampled exposure. This is the role of the matching layer in Figure <a href="#fig:midpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:midpoint">1</a>.

<figure id="fig:midpoint">

<figcaption>A diagonal return is an inner product on the midpoint layer. Both masses use the same exposure, with fresh transitions in each traversal. Coupling either mass to an independent full ideal path gives an expected $ℓ^{1}$ error; diffusion bounds the other mass in $ℓ^{∞}$. The positions are elapsed times, not lattice sites.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-midpoint"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:midpoint`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,scale=0.95]
 \node (left) at (0,0) {$\eta$};
 \node[draw,rounded corners,fill=gray!8,minimum height=9mm,minimum width=20mm]
       (mid) at (5,0) {$\mu_1,\mu_2$};
 \node (right) at (10,0) {$\eta$};
 \draw[->,thick] (left) -- node[above] {$\mathsf C$} (mid);
 \draw[->,thick] (right) -- node[above] {$\mathsf B^*$} (mid);
 \node[below=5mm of left] {$0$};
 \node[above=3mm of mid] {common hole layer};
 \node[below=5mm of mid] {$n\beta/2$};
 \node[below=5mm of right] {$n\beta$};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 31 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A horizontal workflow/timeline diagram showing elapsed time positions at the bottom: 0 at the left, n\beta/2 in the middle, and n\beta at the right. On the left at 0 is an input label \eta, followed by a rightward arrow labeled C leading into a central rounded rectangle containing \mu_1, \mu_2. Above this rounded rectangle is the label "common hole layer". On the right at n\beta is an input label \eta, with a leftward arrow labeled B^* pointing back into the central rounded rectangle.
```


<a id="eq:midpoint-reduction"></a>
<a id="eq:coupling-l1"></a>
Let $G$ be the good-exposure event for $z,R$. Each half has elapsed length $n\beta/2\ge\beta/2$, so Proposition <a href="#prop:diffusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:diffusion">21</a>, including its adjoint statement, gives $\|\mu_1\|_\infty+\|\mu_2\|_\infty\le C\beta^{-3/2}$ on $G$. The ideal halves obey this bound without a good-exposure condition: each slot probability is bounded by its full site probability in <a href="#eq:ideal-site-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ideal-site-bounds">[eq:ideal-site-bounds]</a>. A half need not contain a seam for this upper bound. The elementary pairing inequality gives $$\begin{aligned}
 &\left|\mathbb E_P[(W_R)_{ii}]-(\widehat W_R)_{ii}\right|\notag\\
 &\quad\le C\beta^{-3/2}\mathbb E_P
    \left[\|\mu_1-\widehat\mu_1\|_1+
          \|\mu_2-\widehat\mu_2\|_1\right]
       +\mathbb P_P(G^c).\label{eq:midpoint-reduction}
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed on $G$ subtract the two inner products by changing their first and second arguments successively; on $G^c$ both diagonal entries lie in $[0,1]$. No independence of the two halves is used. Since $\mathbb P_P(G^c)=o_K(\beta^{-3/2})$, it remains to prove $$\label{eq:coupling-l1}
 \mathbb E_P\|\mu_j-\widehat\mu_j\|_1=o_K(1),\qquad j=1,2.$$

*Coupling conditional on the exposure.* Both masses are generated from $i$ along a deterministic schedule of full or partial traversals. For $j=2$, reverse and transpose the suffix factors. We give the coupling for either schedule. Sample the entire actual picture with law $\mathbb P_P$ and expose $\mathcal B_P$. Independently of that entire picture, sample a full unkilled ideal path on the infinite slot lattice, with fresh clocks and seam permutations on each traversal. Its bounded jump rate $\lambda_J$ ensures that it is well defined. Conditional on the exposure, use the same fresh clock on each slot edge whenever its endpoints are holes in the cube. At a prescribed exposed jump, perform the corresponding hole transport. At a seam, share the uniform site permutation if the site has no exposed slots; otherwise use an appropriate fresh uniform completion on its remaining slots. These rules give exactly the hole kernel conditional on the exposure. They also apply to reverse pieces: heat generators are symmetric, transports are inverted, and inverse uniform bijections are uniform. Repeated traversals share the exposure but use independent fresh transitions.

Kill both paths outside $\Lambda^\circ$ or $\mathfrak B_R(z)$. Until they separate these killings agree. There are no interaction bonds from $\Lambda^\circ$ to the exterior of $\Lambda$, by the choice of boundary thickness. A discrepancy before killing therefore requires an exposed slot at a site whose bar-coordinate is within distance $2B_*$ of the full ideal path, at some traversed time. Include both sides of jumps and instantaneous operations in this event. This covers a missing clock edge, a forced transport affecting the common path, and a seam completion different from a full site permutation.

<a id="eq:encounter-initial"></a>
*Averaged encounter bound.* We bound the probability of this encounter unconditionally. Partition the schedule into $O_K(\beta)$ deterministic intervals of elapsed length at most one, each within a single directed traversal piece; assign endpoint operations once. Take $h_\beta=\lceil\log\beta\rceil$. In one such interval let $w$ be the bar-coordinate of the full ideal path at its initial time. Since each jump changes bar-coordinate by at most $B_*$, the probability of displacement greater than $B_*h_\beta$ during the interval is at most <a id="eq:ideal-short-tail"></a>$$\label{eq:ideal-short-tail}
 \frac{C^{h_\beta}}{h_\beta!}.$$ For a deterministic $w$, Corollary <a href="#cor:singleton-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:singleton-bound">27</a>, applied with pin set $P$ and summed over slots, gives $$\label{eq:encounter-initial}
 \mathbb P_P\!\left(
  \text{an exposed slot lies over }
  \mathfrak B_{4B_*(h_\beta+1)}(w)
  \text{ at the initial slice}\right)
 \le C(h_\beta+1)^3\beta^{-3/2}.$$ At a seam the set of sites occupied by exposed slots is the same on both sides, so this estimate covers either endpoint convention.

<a id="eq:encounter-entrance"></a>
If this initial event is absent but an exposed strand subsequently visits $\mathfrak B_{B_*(h_\beta+2)}(w)$ in the interval, trace that strand back toward the initial slice. The distance between the two regions forces at least $h_\beta$ inter-site steps. Count the first $h_\beta$ steps from a visit. Their actual marks are distinct: this interval lies in one traversal, and for large $\beta$ its length is less than $\beta$, so unwrapped physical time is monotone and injective modulo $\beta$. There are at most $C(h_\beta+1)^3$ starting sites in the smaller region and a bounded number of next-site and slot-edge choices per step. By the factorial measure domination of Lemma <a href="#lem:factorial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:factorial">4</a>, the probability of such a list is at most $$\label{eq:encounter-entrance}
 C(h_\beta+1)^3 C^{h_\beta}
  \int_{0<s_1<\cdots<s_{h_\beta}<1}ds_1\cdots ds_{h_\beta}
 =C(h_\beta+1)^3\frac{C^{h_\beta}}{h_\beta!}.$$ Discarding slot compatibility only enlarges the count. A seam can change a slot index but no site, so it merely contributes a bounded slot choice; unwrapping preserves the simplex volume. Reversing a traversal reverses the order of the times and gives the same bound. Thus <a href="#eq:encounter-entrance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:encounter-entrance">[eq:encounter-entrance]</a> uses factorial domination under the full picture law, without conditioning that domination on the exposure.

<a id="eq:encounter-probability"></a>
The random coordinate $w$ depends only on the independent full ideal path. The bounds <a href="#eq:encounter-initial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:encounter-initial">[eq:encounter-initial]</a> and <a href="#eq:encounter-entrance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:encounter-entrance">[eq:encounter-entrance]</a>, uniform for deterministic $w$ and deterministic initial times, therefore apply to this random $w$. A union bound over the intervals bounds the encounter probability by $$\label{eq:encounter-probability}
 C_K\beta(h_\beta+1)^3
       \left(\beta^{-3/2}+\frac{C^{h_\beta}}{h_\beta!}\right)
 =o_K(1).$$ The first term is $O_K(\beta^{-1/2}(\log\beta)^3)$; the factorial term decays faster than every fixed inverse power of $\beta$. Actual marks may reappear in later traversals, but the count used distinctness only within one interval. The union bound needs no independence across these intervals.

Add a death state for killing. Conditional on each exposure, the $\ell^1$ distance between the two live output masses is at most twice the failure probability of this coupling. Average over the picture and use <a href="#eq:encounter-probability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:encounter-probability">[eq:encounter-probability]</a>. This proves <a href="#eq:coupling-l1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coupling-l1">[eq:coupling-l1]</a> for both schedules and, by <a href="#eq:midpoint-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:midpoint-reduction">[eq:midpoint-reduction]</a>, proves <a href="#eq:finite-word" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-word">[eq:finite-word]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Uniform return and down-spin bounds

<a id="stmt-thm-return-bounds"></a>
<div id="thm:return-bounds" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:pin-free-ball"></a>
**Theorem 30** (One-slot bounds with the sharp coefficient). *There is a constant $C<\infty$, independent of $K$, with the following property. For every integer $K\ge1$ there are $\beta_K<\infty$ and a nonnegative function $\epsilon_K(\beta)\to0$ as $\beta\to\infty$, such that for $\beta\ge\beta_K$, every finite cube, every allowed pin set $E$, and every deterministic slot-time point $i$, <a id="eq:down-upper"></a>$$\label{eq:down-upper}
 d_E(i)\le\beta^{-3/2}
 \left(\frac\kappa\ell\sum_{n=1}^{2K-1}n^{-3/2}
                  +CK^{-1/2}+\epsilon_K(\beta)\right).$$ If the site $x$ of $i$ additionally satisfies $$\label{eq:pin-free-ball}
 \mathfrak B_{R_K}(\bar x)\subseteq\Lambda^\circ,
 \qquad E\text{ has no pin over }\mathfrak B_{R_K}(\bar x),$$ then <a id="eq:down-lower"></a>$$\label{eq:down-lower}
 d_E(i)\ge\beta^{-3/2}
 \left(\frac\kappa\ell\sum_{n=1}^{K}n^{-3/2}
                                      -\epsilon_K(\beta)\right).$$ For $i\notin E$, the same two bounds hold for $r(E,\{i\})$, with the same choice of error function. The threshold and error function are independent of the cube, cut, slot, and number, locations, and times of all finite extra pins. Only the fixed model and geometric choices, and $K$, may affect them.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-return-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-return-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose first that $i\notin E$, and take the exposure law $\mathbb P_P$, $P=E\cup\{i\}$. Lemma <a href="#lem:return-expansion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:return-expansion">25</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:long-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:long-tail">26</a> give $$r(E,\{i\})\le
 \sum_{n=1}^{2K-1}\mathbb E_P\langle\eta,Q^n\eta\rangle
 +\beta^{-3/2}\bigl(CK^{-1/2}+o_K(1)\bigr).$$ Expand $Q^n$ as the equally weighted average of the $2^n$ direction words. Replacing each word by its localized version costs at most $C_Ke^{-c\beta}$ in expected diagonal mass, by Lemma <a href="#lem:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:localization">20</a>. Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-word" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-word">29</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:ideal-kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ideal-kernel">28</a> then imply $$r(E,\{i\})\le\beta^{-3/2}
 \left(\frac\kappa\ell\sum_{n=1}^{2K-1}n^{-3/2}
                   +CK^{-1/2}+o_K(1)\right).$$ There are only finitely many words at fixed $K$, so all comparison errors enter $o_K(1)$. They do not alter the constant multiplying $K^{-1/2}$.

<a id="eq:bonferroni"></a>
For the lower bound assume <a href="#eq:pin-free-ball" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pin-free-ball">[eq:pin-free-ball]</a>. At fixed exposure, run the forward fresh walk with boundary and ball killing, and continue it after visits to $i$. Let $A_n$ be the event that it is alive and at $i$ at elapsed time $n\beta$. Before any such visit it has stayed in the ball and hence avoided $E$. Thus $q_i\ge\Pr(\bigcup_{n=1}^K A_n\mid\text{exposure})$. The fresh transitions on successive turns give $$\Pr(A_n\mid\text{exposure})=(U_{+,R}^n)_{ii},\qquad
 \Pr(A_j\cap A_n\mid\text{exposure})
     =(U_{+,R}^j)_{ii}(U_{+,R}^{n-j})_{ii}\quad(j<n).$$ The Bonferroni lower bound therefore reads $$\label{eq:bonferroni}
 q_i\ge\sum_{n=1}^K(U_{+,R}^n)_{ii}
 -\sum_{1\le j<n\le K}
       (U_{+,R}^j)_{ii}(U_{+,R}^{n-j})_{ii}.$$ On good exposures each product is $O(\beta^{-3})$ by Proposition <a href="#prop:diffusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:diffusion">21</a>; on the complement each is at most one, and that complement has probability $o_K(\beta^{-3/2})$. The expected pair sum is consequently $o_K(\beta^{-3/2})$. For the first sum apply Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-word" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-word">29</a> to the all-forward word and use the interior comparison <a href="#eq:ideal-killed-interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ideal-killed-interior">[eq:ideal-killed-interior]</a>. Averaging <a href="#eq:bonferroni" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bonferroni">[eq:bonferroni]</a> gives $$r(E,\{i\})\ge\beta^{-3/2}
 \left(\frac\kappa\ell\sum_{n=1}^{K}n^{-3/2}-o_K(1)\right).$$

Finally, Proposition <a href="#prop:pin-identities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pin-identities">5</a> and Corollary <a href="#cor:singleton-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:singleton-bound">27</a> give, uniformly, $$d_E(i)=\frac{r(E,\{i\})}{1+r(E,\{i\})},\qquad
 0\le r(E,\{i\})-d_E(i)\le C\beta^{-3}.$$ This changes only the normalized $o_K(1)$ errors. Choose a common threshold $\beta_K$ and a nonnegative error function tending to zero that dominates all these uniform error magnitudes. This proves both estimates for $r$ and $d$. If $i\in E$, then $d_E(i)=0$, so the upper bound still holds; the lower-bound hypotheses already exclude this case. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:pressure"></a>
### From pinned returns to spontaneous magnetization

The return estimates hold for every deterministic choice of extra pins. A positive magnetic field is an exact mixture of such choices. We use this fact to bound finite-volume pressure increments, then take the thermodynamic limit before differentiating at zero field.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Pressure and the cost of boundary pins

Write $V=|\Lambda_N|$ and $$p_N(h)=\frac1{\beta V}\log Z_{\Lambda_N}(h).$$ The limit $p_\beta(h)=\lim_{N\to\infty}p_N(h)$ exists for every $\beta>0$ and $h\in\mathbb R$. Here is the finite-range argument. Tile a large cube, apart from a remainder, by cubes of a fixed side $l$ and delete all interactions between tiles. There are $O(V/l)$ deleted bonds, each with uniformly bounded operator norm; the remainder has $o(V)$ sites as $N\to\infty$ with $l$ fixed and may be left as singletons. If two Hamiltonians differ by an operator of norm at most $a$, their logarithmic partition functions differ by at most $\beta a$, by ordered-eigenvalue comparison. Consequently the limiting upper and lower pressures both lie within $C/l$ of the pressure per site of a single tile. Letting $l\to\infty$ proves existence. Since $[H^J_{S,N},M_{S,N}]=0$ and $\norm{M_{S,N}}\le SV$, each $p_N$ is convex and $S$-Lipschitz in $h$. These properties pass to $p_\beta$, so its right derivative at zero exists.

Let $Z^+_\Lambda(h)$ be the loop expression in Proposition <a href="#prop:loop-representation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:loop-representation">2</a> with all cycles meeting $D_0$ constrained to have color $+1$, and put $p_N^+(h)=(\beta V)^{-1}\log Z^+_{\Lambda_N}(h)$.

<a id="stmt-prop-pressure-equivalence"></a>
<div id="prop:pressure-equivalence" class="proposition">

**Proposition 31** (Boundary pressure equivalence). *For every fixed $\beta>0$ and $h\ge0$, <a id="eq:boundary-pressure"></a>$$\lim_{N\to\infty}p_N^+(h)=p_\beta(h).
 \label{eq:boundary-pressure}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-pressure-equivalence"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-pressure-equivalence</summary>

<a id="eq:surface-cost"></a>
*Proof.* Factor $e^{\beta hSV}$ from the unrestricted color sum. Its picture weight, relative to the base law, becomes $$W_h=\prod_C(1+e^{-\beta h a_C}).$$ Given the picture, the colors of different cycles are independent and the probability that a cycle is up is at least $1/2$. If $b_\partial$ counts the cycles meeting $D_0$, the corresponding normalized picture expectation therefore satisfies $$1\ge \frac{Z^+_\Lambda(h)}{Z_\Lambda(h)}
 \ge \mathbb E_h[2^{-b_\partial}]
 \ge \exp\{-\log(2)\,\mathbb E_h b_\partial\}.
 \label{eq:surface-cost}$$ A cycle visiting the boundary either contains a time-zero boundary slot or arrives there at a mark incident to the boundary. Thus $$b_\partial\le \ell|\partial\Lambda|+2N_\partial,$$ where $N_\partial$ is the number of such marks. Insertion of a mark changes at most two cycle factors in $W_h$, each between one and two, so it increases $W_h$ by at most a factor of four. The Poisson insertion argument of Lemma <a href="#lem:factorial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:factorial">4</a> gives $\mathbb E_hN_\partial\le C\beta|\partial\Lambda|$. Taking logarithms in <a href="#eq:surface-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:surface-cost">[eq:surface-cost]</a> yields $$0\le p_N(h)-p_N^+(h)
 \le C(1+\beta)\frac{|\partial\Lambda|}{\beta|\Lambda|}.$$ For fixed $\beta$, the right side tends to zero. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### A magnetic field as random extra pins

The mixture in the next lemma is useful because its conditional laws are precisely the deterministic pin laws already studied. Its mixing measure need not be a product measure.

<a id="stmt-lem-pin-mixture"></a>
<div id="lem:pin-mixture" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:pin-marginal"></a>
<a id="eq:field-deficit"></a>
**Lemma 32** (Extra-pin mixture). *For $h\ge0$, the colored-picture law defining $Z^+_\Lambda(h)$ is a mixture of the zero-field pinned laws with pin sets $E=D_0\cup F$, where $F$ consists of time-zero slots. Under the mixing law, $$\Pr(i\in F)\le b(h):=1-e^{-\beta h}
 \qquad\text{for every time-zero slot }i.
 \label{eq:pin-marginal}$$ Moreover, $$S-(p_N^+)'(h)
   =\frac1V\sum_{i\in V_\Lambda}\mathbb E_F[d_{D_0\cup F}(i)].
 \label{eq:field-deficit}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-pin-mixture"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-pin-mixture</summary>

*Proof.* For a colored picture let $D_{\rm down}$ count its down slots at time zero. Then $M=SV-D_{\rm down}$. Start with independent selection of all time-zero slots with probability $b(h)$. The probability that only up slots are selected is $(1-b(h))^{D_{\rm down}}=e^{-\beta hD_{\rm down}}$. Consequently the joint weight obtained by multiplying the zero-field colored-picture weight by the independent selection weight and by $\mathbf 1_{F\text{ contains only up slots}}$ has exactly the desired field-tilted colored-picture marginal, after normalization. Conditioning instead on $F$ leaves unit weight for each coloring satisfying the pins $D_0\cup F$. Summing these colorings gives the picture factor $2^{n_{D_0\cup F}}$, as required.

Conditional on the picture and its colors in this joint law, down slots are never selected, while up slots are selected independently with probability $b(h)$. This proves <a href="#eq:pin-marginal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pin-marginal">[eq:pin-marginal]</a>. Finally, differentiation of the finite-volume field tilt gives $(p_N^+)'(h)=V^{-1}\mathbb E M$; conditioning on $F$ gives <a href="#eq:field-deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:field-deficit">[eq:field-deficit]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### The order of limits

<a id="eq:pressure-upper"></a>
Recall the ideal heat-kernel constant and define its finite sums by <a id="eq:partial-bloch"></a>$$\kappa=\frac1{(4\pi)^{3/2}\sqrt{\det D_{S,J}}},
 \qquad c_j=\kappa\sum_{n=1}^j n^{-3/2}.
 \label{eq:partial-bloch}$$ Fix an integer $K\ge1$ and use the radius $R=R_K=\lceil a(K+1)\beta\rceil$ from <a href="#eq:scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scales">[eq:scales]</a>. The upper down-probability bound of Theorem <a href="#thm:return-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:return-bounds">30</a> and <a href="#eq:field-deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:field-deficit">[eq:field-deficit]</a> imply $$S-(p_N^+)'(h)
 \le\beta^{-3/2}
       \bigl(c_{2K-1}+CK^{-1/2}+\epsilon_K(\beta)\bigr),
 \label{eq:pressure-upper}$$ where $\epsilon_K(\beta)\ge0$ tends to zero as $\beta\to\infty$ for fixed $K$, uniformly in $N$ and $h\ge0$. The leading constant $C$ is independent of $K$. There are $\ell V$ slots in the sum, so the factor $1/\ell$ in each ideal slot-return probability has canceled.

<a id="eq:pressure-lower"></a>
For a lower bound, let $\rho_N$ be the fraction of sites $x\in\Lambda_N$ for which $\mathfrak B_R(\bar x)\subseteq\Lambda_N^\circ$. Since the coset representatives and $A$ are fixed, all sites in this ball lie within a bounded physical distance of $x$ when $R$ is fixed. Hence $\rho_N\to1$ as $N\to\infty$, with $\beta,K$ fixed. There are at most $$v_R=\ell|\mathfrak C|(2R+1)^3$$ time-zero slots above the ball. The union bound and <a href="#eq:pin-marginal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pin-marginal">[eq:pin-marginal]</a> show that it contains no extra pin with probability at least $[1-v_Rb(h)]_+$, where $[u]_+=\max\{u,0\}$. On this event Theorem <a href="#thm:return-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:return-bounds">30</a> supplies its lower bound. Enlarging $\epsilon_K$ to cover both errors gives $$S-(p_N^+)'(h)
 \ge\rho_N[1-v_Rb(h)]_+\,
       \beta^{-3/2}\bigl(c_K-\epsilon_K(\beta)\bigr).
 \label{eq:pressure-lower}$$ Here and below $\beta$ is sufficiently large, depending on $K$, that $c_K-\epsilon_K(\beta)\ge0$.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-pin-mixture-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-pin-mixture</summary>

<a id="eq:final-squeeze"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Integrate <a href="#eq:pressure-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pressure-upper">[eq:pressure-upper]</a> and <a href="#eq:pressure-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pressure-lower">[eq:pressure-lower]</a> from $0$ to $h>0$, and divide by $h$. This bounds $$S-\frac{p_N^+(h)-p_N^+(0)}{h}.$$ First send $N\to\infty$, using Proposition <a href="#prop:pressure-equivalence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pressure-equivalence">31</a> at both endpoints and $\rho_N\to1$. Then send $h\downarrow0$. For these fixed $\beta,K$, continuity of $b$ at zero gives $$\lim_{h\downarrow0}\frac1h\int_0^h[1-v_Rb(u)]_+\,du=1.$$ By the definition of the right derivative, $$c_K-\epsilon_K(\beta)
 \le\beta^{3/2}\bigl(S-m_{S,J}(\beta)\bigr)
 \le c_{2K-1}+CK^{-1/2}+\epsilon_K(\beta).
 \label{eq:final-squeeze}$$ Thus the thermodynamic limit and the zero-field derivative have been handled through pressure increments. Now let $\beta\to\infty$ with $K$ fixed and then $K\to\infty$. Both sides of <a href="#eq:final-squeeze" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-squeeze">[eq:final-squeeze]</a> converge to $\kappa\zeta(3/2)$, proving the stated law. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "A diagonal return is an inner product on the midpoint layer. Both masses use the same exposure, with fresh transitions in each traversal. Coupling either mass to an independent full ideal path gives an expected $ℓ^{1}$ error; diffusion bounds the other mass in $ℓ^{∞}$. The positions are elapsed times, not lattice sites.",
      "figure_id": "fig:midpoint",
      "pdf_page": 31,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:midpoint",
          "exact_quote": "A diagonal return is an inner product on the midpoint layer.\nBoth masses use the same exposure, with fresh transitions in each\ntraversal. Coupling either mass to an independent full ideal path gives\nan expected \\(\\ell^1\\) error; diffusion b",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,scale=0.95]\n \\node (left) at (0,0) {$\\eta$};\n \\node[draw,rounded corners,fill=gray!8,minimum height=9mm,minimum width=20mm]\n       (mid) at (5,0) {$\\mu_1,\\mu_2$};\n \\node (right) at (10,0) {$\\eta$};\n \\draw[->,thick] (left) -- node[above] {$\\mathsf C$} (mid);\n \\draw[->,thick] (right) -- node[above] {$\\mathsf B^*$} (mid);\n \\node[below=5mm of left] {$0$};\n \\node[above=3mm of mid] {common hole layer};\n \\node[below=5mm of mid] {$n\\beta/2$};\n \\node[below=5mm of right] {$n\\beta$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A horizontal workflow/timeline diagram showing elapsed time positions at the bottom: 0 at the left, n\\beta/2 in the middle, and n\\beta at the right. On the left at 0 is an input label \\eta, followed by a rightward arrow labeled C leading into a central rounded rectangle containing \\mu_1, \\mu_2. Above this rounded rectangle is the label \"common hole layer\". On the right at n\\beta is an input label \\eta, with a leftward arrow labeled B^* pointing back into the central rounded rectangle."
    }
  ],
